#3010. 3 AI Hardware Stocks Down More Than 30% From Their Records to Buy Now and Hold Through 2030

Each of these AI suppliers sits well below its record close. Has data center demand actually cooled that much?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 22, 2026 at 4:43PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -14.01%), Coherent (COHR -12.90%) and Dell Technologies (DELL -11.04%) all supply the same artificial intelligence (AI) build-out. Nvidia makes the chips, Coherent makes optical parts that carry data between them, and Dell builds the servers they go into.

All three stocks have also tumbled. Nvidia closed at a record $239.24 on Oct. 6 and trades around $153 as I write this, about 36% lower. Coherent, near $223, is off about 48% from its June 2 record close. And Dell, roughly $322, is about 45% under the $588.40 record close it set on Sept. 17.

But their businesses haven't followed the stocks down. Each company's latest report shows data center demand still growing, and I think each stock now leaves room for gains through 2030.

On Nov. 11, Nvidia's board accepted Jensen Huang's resignation as CEO and made Colette Kress, the chief financial officer, interim CEO while it looks for a permanent one.

The business, though, kept speeding up. In the fiscal third quarter of 2027, which ended Oct. 25, 2026, Nvidia's data center revenue hit $107.6 billion. It was 21% above the previous quarter, and sequential growth accelerated from 18% in fiscal Q2. Management expects around $130 billion in total revenue for the fiscal fourth quarter, but that period has an extra week.

Shares still dropped about 3% the day after the Nov. 17 report. Using analysts' average forecast for fiscal 2028 (the year ending January 2028), the stock costs under 10 times earnings, while the forward price-to-earnings ratio of the S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%) is around 18.

The CEO search might keep some investors wary for a while. But for a business whose data center sales more than doubled year over year, I think the stock is cheap.

Coherent's lasers and optical transceivers move data between chips inside a data center, and between data centers.

Showing how much AI now powers the company, Coherent's datacenter and communications segment took in $1.95 billion in its fiscal first quarter of 2027 (which ended Sept. 30, 2026), or 82% of total revenue. Segment sales rose 79% year over year and 21% sequentially, up from $1.09 billion a year before and $1.62 billion in fiscal Q4 2026.

For a 2030 holder, the longer deals might matter more. Coherent signed one for its new PhotonLink optics platform worth around $20 billion over five years, with revenue starting to ramp in early 2028. Also, on Nov. 18 the board approved a share buyback program of up to $2 billion, around 5% of Coherent's market value at the current stock price.

Granted, operating cash flow was negative $93 million last quarter, and Coherent spent another $776 million on new equipment and facilities to expand capacity. And at around 22 times earnings using analysts' fiscal 2027 forecasts (that year ends in June 2027), it's the priciest of the three. Still, with management expecting pro forma revenue growth (which leaves out businesses it sold) over 60% this fiscal year, I'd call that valuation reasonable.

Dell's AI server orders are arriving faster than Dell is shipping them. In its fiscal second quarter of 2027 (which ended July 31, 2026), the company booked a record $60.9 billion in AI server orders, almost four times the $16.4 billion in AI server revenue it recorded. It closed the period with a record $95 billion AI server backlog.

Total revenue rose 58% year over year to $47.0 billion. And with management guiding to non-GAAP (adjusted) earnings per share of $25.50 this fiscal year, the stock trades at around 13 times those earnings.

But profits have outpaced cash. Dell's operating cash flow dropped 13% year over year to $2.2 billion in fiscal Q2, even as net income more than tripled to $4.1 billion. A big reason is financing receivables (money customers owe Dell under financing deals), which cut operating cash flow by around $6.7 billion in the quarter. The fiscal third-quarter report on Tuesday, Nov. 24, might show whether more of that cash is starting to come in.

All three stocks might continue to slide together if AI spending cools. But each company is growing fast, and I think each trades at a valuation that leaves room for that growth to reach shareholders by 2030.

I'd start with Nvidia, the cheapest of the three relative to its expected earnings and the one whose data center growth accelerated last quarter. Coherent would be next, with its multiyear PhotonLink deal. Dell is worth a look too, but I'd keep the position smaller until its cash flow catches up to its profits.

Atmos Rewards offers six communities, each with its own travel perks and opportunities to earn bonus points

Atmos Rewards is the airline loyalty program for Alaska Airlines and Hawaiian Airlines. One of its newer features is Atmos Communities, which offer additional rewards and benefits tailored to different travel interests.

There are six communities, including two reserved for Alaska and Hawaii residents. The other four are open to members regardless of where they live. But don’t get caught up in the community names. The best choice for you may depend more on your travel plans than the groupings suggest.

Here’s how to decide which Atmos Community best fits your travel patterns.

Atmos Communities are like groups within the Atmos Rewards program that offer additional benefits based on different travel interests.

They’re free to join for Atmos Rewards members. Four communities are available to all members, while community Club 49 is reserved for Alaska residents and community Huaka’i by Hawaiian is reserved for Hawaii residents.

You can belong to only one community at a time and switch communities once per calendar year. Consider your upcoming travel plans before choosing.

Joining a community is easy: Log in to your Atmos Rewards account and navigate to “Account overview.” Select “Join a Community,” browse the options and click the “Join” button under the one you choose.

Each Atmos Community has a list of 10 destinations where members can earn bonus points and status points. Although the destinations differ by community, the rewards structure is the same.

Through Dec. 31, 2027, members can earn up to 10,000 bonus points and 10,000 status points by visiting qualifying destinations.

Here’s how many points you’ll earn as you reach each milestone:

Visit one destination, earn 100 status and bonus points.

Visit two destinations, earn 200 status and bonus points.

Visit three destinations, earn 300 status and bonus points.

Visit four destinations, earn 400 status and bonus points.

Visit five destinations, earn 500 status and bonus points.

Visit six destinations, earn 750 status and bonus points.

Visit seven destinations, earn 1,000 status and bonus points.

Visit eight destinations, earn 1,250 status and bonus points.

Visit nine destinations, earn 2,500 status and bonus points.

Visit 10 destinations, earn 3,000 status and bonus points.

The first member of each community to visit all 10 qualifying destinations during the challenge period will also earn 100,000 bonus points.

New lists of qualifying destinations for each community take effect on Jan. 1, 2028.

Here’s a closer look at the six communities, their benefits and the destinations eligible for bonus points.

Global Locals is geared toward frequent international travelers. Benefits include:

Earn 10% more status points toward Atmos elite status on flights that begin and/or end outside the U.S., including award flights.

Earn 1 status point for every 25 points transferred to Atmos Rewards from Bilt or Marriott Bonvoy.

Get 20% off Atmos Rewards Unlocked experiences.

Through Dec. 31, 2027, these 10 Oneworld Alliance hubs count toward bonus points and status points. Qualifying layovers also count toward the destination challenge, so you don’t necessarily need to make these cities your final stop.

If you frequently travel internationally or have any upcoming flights through any of these airports, including qualifying layovers, Global Locals might be a fit for you.

If you have the Atmos™ Rewards Summit Visa Infinite® credit card, you’ll earn 3 Atmos points per $1 spent on eligible Alaska and Hawaiian Airlines purchases, dining and foreign transactions. The last bonus category can be especially useful for frequent international travelers.

This community has a similar setup to Global Locals, but most of its bonus destinations are in the U.S. Here’s what you can get:

Get 20% off Atmos Rewards Unlocked experiences.

Get 20% of your points back on eligible award flights for kids 12 and under after you complete your trip.

Two free checked bags when traveling to or from Alaska on Alaska Airlines or Hawaiian Airlines.

Three free checked bags when traveling within Alaska on Alaska Airlines.

Hawaii residents have their own community just for them: Huaka’i by Hawaiian. It offers benefits focused on interisland travel:

Don’t be persuaded by the names of the communities. Look a little deeper, because there’s a lot of overlap between destination lists. Compare the options against your upcoming travel plans.

You can change communities once per calendar year, so you aren’t locked in to your initial choice indefinitely. However, switching communities resets your destination-challenge progress, but you’ll retain any points you’ve already earned.

If your home airport (or one you visit frequently) is already on a community’s list, you may have an easier time earning points. Also consider whether a particular community benefit, such as free checked bags or points back on award flights for kids, outweighs the destination challenge.

Finally, pay attention to airport codes. For example, Tokyo’s Narita (NRT) and Haneda (HND) appear on different community lists. New York’s qualifying airport is JFK, while it is DCA in Washington, D.C. If you have a choice between airports, check which one qualifies for your community’s challenge.

#3007. SI-BONE: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Higher hospital reimbursement rates for complex spinal surgeries could accelerate adoption.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 22, 2026 at 4:57PM EST

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 2, 2026, SI-BONE (SIBN -0.08%) announced a 15% increase in worldwide revenue to $55.7 million, driven by expanding adoption of its surgical implants. The medical device maker is extending its anatomical fixation products into wider spinal applications, positioning itself to capture a larger share of complex surgeries.

Management launched iFuse Granite Onyx, a medical device designed to fix spinal fusion failures, following clearance from the Food and Drug Administration (FDA). This product extends the company's addressable market by targeting procedures that suffer from high failure rates.

Updated Medicare severity diagnosis-related groups (DRGs), which define fixed payment rates for hospital stays, took effect in October to cover complex spinal surgeries. Higher reimbursement reduces financial hurdles for surgical centers, paving the way for wider platform adoption.

The commercial network expanded to 1,770 active doctors performing procedures, representing a 16% increase year over year. A broader physician footprint gives the company immediate access to cross-sell new device lines without expanding overhead at the same rate.

Procedure demand is accelerating across surgical centers, and executive leadership is displaying clear momentum.

The company relies on custom implant engineering and established training networks to shield its platform from rivals. Management is keeping close watch on pricing discipline and sales productivity as product launches roll out.

#3005. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 22, 2026 at 5:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Supernus Pharmaceuticals ›

Steady cash flow generation provides the financial capacity to advance clinical research and expand market outreach.

#3000. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, November 20: Noticeably Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased today, but borrowing costs are still expensive for many home buyers.

Mortgage rates dropped today, but anything above 7% still puts pressure on home buyers’ budgets.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.24% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and three basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

A significant drop is eye-catching, but you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Next week, the Nerds are waiting for the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index, due out Wednesday. It's the Federal Reserve's preferred measure of inflation, so investors will be watching closely. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but lenders anticipate its decisions, so mortgage rates often move before the Fed acts. Stubbornly high inflation is a big reason why we’re seeing upward pressure on mortgage rates right now.

If PCE shows inflation comes in hotter than expected, mortgage rates could climb as markets anticipate further Fed rate hikes. (An increase to the federal funds rate is already widely expected at the Fed’s Dec. 8-9 meeting.) A cooler reading could ease some pressure on mortgage rates for now.

I’m not getting my hopes up too high for meaningfully lower inflation, though. Last week's Producer Price Index showed wholesale inflation climbed 6.5% over the past year — its fastest pace in four years. Wholesale inflation measures the price businesses pay for things. When businesses’ costs go up, they could pass those along to consumers in the form of higher prices — keeping the flames of inflation alive.

Whether Wednesday's PCE brings relief or another helping of bad inflation news, we'll know before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.74% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

The rate you see advertised is a sample rate — usually for a borrower with perfect credit, making a big down payment, and paying for mortgage points. That won't match every buyer's circumstances.

In addition to market factors outside of your control, your customized quote depends on your:

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.

» Get the best rate for you: How to get the best mortgage rate

Maybe — but even personalized rate quotes can change until you lock. That’s because lenders adjust pricing multiple times a day in response to market changes.

#2998. Should You Buy Tesla or SpaceX Stock Before Their Potential Merger?

The potential combination has a clear winner.

By Adam Levy – Nov 22, 2026 at 5:01PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

While it's long been rumored that Elon Musk could move to combine Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -7.69%) and Tesla (TSLA -7.64%), the megamerger is closer to a reality than it's ever been. The two companies are in advanced discussions, and a merger announcement could come as soon as next month, according to Bloomberg.

The new company would combine SpaceX's artificial intelligence capabilities and infrastructure with Tesla's physical AI (self-driving cars and robots), potentially benefiting from vertical integration and scale. Investors considering an investment in either Tesla or SpaceX ahead of any merger announcement need to consider what they stand to gain from a deal and what they're giving up.

A merger of Tesla would be an all-stock deal with SpaceX paying Tesla shareholders in stock to absorb the company. That dilutes existing SpaceX shareholders' stock, but they end up owning a piece of a larger company.

Tesla shareholders could benefit in the short term. Acquisitions usually require the acquiring company to pay a premium for the acquired company’s shareholders to agree to sell their shares. But that premium can quickly disappear in an all-stock deal. If the market thinks the acquiring company overpaid, it can send the share price lower well ahead of the merger closing, decreasing the actual value received.

Furthermore, Tesla's share price will become pegged to SpaceX's performance until the deal closes. Anyone considering owning Tesla stock needs to be comfortable with the idea that SpaceX’s financials will impact its share price.

That's a key question, given that SpaceX stock is currently priced for significant revenue growth over the next few years. It holds major contracts with multiple AI companies to sell infrastructure at a premium price and plans to add a whopping 10 gigawatts of capacity in 2027, building on the 1.7 gigawatts it ended the third quarter with. Achieving that expansion and selling its full capacity while maintaining its high pricing would result in phenomenal growth, but that two-step process is far from guaranteed.

At the same time, investors need to be comfortable with the fact that even if SpaceX and Tesla agree to a merger, it might not receive approval. SpaceX is a contractor with the U.S. government. Tesla's ties to China -- its Shanghai plant built more than half its vehicles in 2025 -- could deter regulators from approving the deal.

That said, other U.S. defense contractors have been able to maintain operations in China, maintaining a firewall between different segments. However, that could limit the potential synergies between SpaceX and Tesla.

Finally, SpaceX buyers have to be comfortable with acquiring Tesla. The majority of the auto company's value is based on the potential of its fledgling robotaxi business and its humanoid robotics. Neither generates any meaningful revenue at this point, and Musk has been prone to overpromising and underdelivering on Tesla's real-world AI implementations.

While a merger has potential benefits and drawbacks for regular Tesla and SpaceX shareholders, Musk would be the clear winner. Any merger agreement will likely ensure Musk maintains majority control over the vote at the combined company. That means Tesla shareholders have to be comfortable with Musk having the freedom to practically do whatever he wants with the business.

To be sure, both Tesla and SpaceX investors are already likely big fans of Musk. He has full control over SpaceX, and Tesla shareholders eventually approved a $1 trillion pay package for Musk last year, as he threatened to leave the company if voters didn't pass it. Musk previously posted on X that he wants at least 25% voting control at Tesla, "enough to be influential, but not so much that I can't be overturned." If the merger goes through, however, he will have enough control to avoid being overturned.

This might not be a big issue to most shareholders, but it's certainly something to consider. There are many public companies where the CEO controls the vote. But Tesla shareholders will be giving up any checks they previously had on Musk. When you combine that with the premium price both stocks currently trade at, it's hard to recommend buying either stock ahead of the potential merger.

#2991. College Scholarship Breakdown: Types, Sponsors and Eligibility at a Glance

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Scholarships vary in what they pay for and who can apply. Understanding the differences can save you time during your application process.

Scholarships have become a standard part of applying for college. You can learn about scholarships in a variety of ways, but looking through an online database of available scholarships — the most common way — can be overwhelming.

Before you go searching for a scholarship, it's important to understand the types of scholarships available and how to identify the ones that give you the best chance of winning, which can save you time and potentially earn you more funding. Here’s what you need to know about scholarships to decide which ones are best for you.

Scholarships are often funded by three groups: private organizations, institutions like universities, and government agencies. None is better than the other when it comes to awarding funds.

Nonprofit organizations and clubs that are looking for students either majoring in or researching certain fields, or who have experience volunteering in a specific field.

Companies that want to support all students or only fund those majoring in a field that relates to the companies’ industries.

Financial institutions, such as banks or credit unions, that want to support their community or their members.

Individuals or families that are funding private scholarships, usually in memory of a loved one.

Universities and colleges that are offering funds to students who attend their campuses.

Research organizations that want to fund students who will research in their areas of interest and further their causes.

These scholarships generally are provided by local, state or federal government agencies for a variety of reasons. Despite being funded by a government agency, the scholarships do not require you to pay back the money — unlike federal student loans.

Some scholarships pay for anything related to college expenses, including tuition, room and board, books and supplies, and more. Other scholarships limit what you can pay for with the funds, such as only tuition or tuition along with books. It’s important to look at this restriction when applying for scholarships to ensure the funds will go toward what you need to cover.

Merit scholarships require you to show excellence or achievements in your schoolwork, through talents and abilities, and even leadership or service work. Because these types of awards can be highly competitive, merit scholarships might require more than just achievements, such as volunteer or leadership experience. This means merit-based scholarships can overlap with other types of scholarship categories. Here are some examples of merit-based scholarships:

Merit scholarships often require you to have earned a minimum grade point average (GPA). The minimum GPA depends on the scholarship but often ranges from 2.0 to 4.0, with more competitive scholarships usually requiring higher GPAs. Scholarships with lower GPA minimums are more likely to consider additional factors, such as financial need.

Some merit scholarships might require a minimum ACT or SAT score instead of a GPA — or in addition to a minimum GPA.

Merit scholarships might also require applicants to have participated in activities outside of their schoolwork. This is common for scholarships that require membership in a certain group or organization. For example, membership in agricultural clubs, such as 4-H or FFA, and scouting groups, such as the National Eagle Scout Association, appear in scholarship requirements.

Some scholarships emphasize volunteering or community service and only consider applicants who have that experience. Applications vary in how detailed their requirements can be. Some scholarships want only a general description of where and how you’ve volunteered, and others might require experience with a service project that shows more leadership experience or time volunteering with a certain type of organization.

Scholarships that are awarded to students who can’t afford college are called need-based scholarships.

Unmet financial need is the difference between what it will cost you to attend college and what you can afford to pay. Because calculating how much you can afford is a complicated process, many scholarships refer to your FAFSA to document your financial need.

Some scholarship requirements may be as straightforward as demonstrating that you have financial need, but some scholarships might have income limits. However, income restrictions are less common.

Athletic scholarships are for — you guessed it — athletes. They are usually offered by colleges and universities to recruit athletes onto their collegiate teams and can be competitive. Which sports have scholarships available and the scholarship amounts vary by college.

Even though the main criterion for an athletic scholarship is being good at your sport, you’ll likely still need to show that you can keep up with your schoolwork. Most athletic scholarships require students to earn a minimum GPA or test scores to remain eligible while they’re in college.

Some scholarships are designated for students majoring in a general field, such as science or education, but others can be for very specific areas of study. For example, a scholarship for students majoring in a natural resource field could include majors in biology, ecology, wildlife biology, conservation education, forestry, soils and hydrology. However, a stricter scholarship might state that students have to major in more specific topics, such as biochemistry or microbial ecology.

Scholarships are available for active and former members of the military and members of their families. In addition to federal funds available for veterans to attend college, private organizations and some universities have scholarships for veterans to offset the cost of a degree.

Which family members can apply for funds depends on the individual scholarship. For example, some scholarships extend to children or spouses of veterans, but some go as far in a family tree as great-grandchildren.

Veteran- and military-focused scholarships might require that applicants or their family members enlisted in a certain branch, served during a particular time or conflict, or meet a certain rank or status such as receiving disability. Scholarships might also require that the applicant be a wartime veteran or family member of a wartime veteran; not all veterans meet this status.

Scholarships are also awarded to people who identify with a certain group. Common examples of identity-based scholarships include those for people who have disabilities, belong to certain ethnic or religious groups, identify as LGBTQIA+ or are first-generation students. Some identity-based scholarships require applicants to meet several criteria, such as belonging to more than one identity group.

Outside of what would be considered standard DEI groups, there are scholarships for applicants who identify with less common groups, such as veganism, homelessness and model railroad builders.

The best scholarships for you depend on your situation. For example, if you are a skilled athlete with a good academic record, going for an athletic scholarship might give you the most funding. On the other hand, if you’re going to research a topic that isn’t common, looking for scholarships that support your research topic might be your best bet because competition could be lower.

Good tactics to figure out which scholarships are worth your time include:

Think of the skills, talents, experience and identities that are unique to you. For example, being a child of a veteran immediately qualifies you for scholarships with fewer applicants than scholarships open to everyone. Likewise, if you embrace a certain ethnicity, identify with a specific group or have experience with a type of work, you might qualify for scholarships that appreciate and recognize those attributes more than the average scholarship.

Apply if you think you're eligible. Don’t apply to just a few scholarships and hope you get them. Instead, apply for as many scholarships as you can and for which you’re eligible. This increases your chances of receiving funds. Even small scholarships can make a big impact when you add several of them together.

Understand how you can use the money. If you already have a scholarship that covers only books, applying for the same type of scholarship isn’t helpful if you don’t need the extra funds. Instead, look for scholarships that cover the costs you have remaining.

Not sure where to look? Our sister site Scholarships360.org provides students with vetted scholarship opportunities. That would be a good place to start.

#2976. Ford Motor vs. General Motors: Which Automotive Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Ford's commercial arm is steadying cash flow, but GM's higher profitability tells a different story for 2027.

By Sara Appino – Updated Nov 22, 2026 at 5:15PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Ford Motor Company ›

As the transition to electric vehicles tests the resilience of legacy automakers, Ford Motor (F -0.62%) and General Motors (GM -3.72%) are racing to dominate the driveway. Which stock is the better buy for your portfolio?

Ford is doubling down on its commercial segment through Ford Pro, while General Motors relies on a massive brand portfolio and its OnStar ecosystem to maintain loyalty. Both companies face the capital-intensive challenge of pivoting away from traditional engines. Comparing their financials and risk profiles helps determine which legacy giant is better positioned for the road ahead.

Ford is restructuring its operations into three distinct units to better manage its evolution into a technology-led company. As a major player among consumer discretionary stocks, the company serves retail buyers through its gas-powered Ford Blue and electric Ford Model e segments. However, the Ford Pro division is a central pillar of its strategy, focusing on fleet optimization and telematics for commercial and government clients. This division provides a steady stream of recurring revenue from software services and maintenance for business fleets.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $187.0 billion, which represents a decrease of approximately 1.4% compared to the prior period. During this time, the automaker reported a net loss of close to $7.8 billion. This negative bottom-line result reflects the heavy costs associated with its aggressive capital investment plan and the ongoing expenses of scaling its electric vehicle production. Despite the top-line dip, the company continues to rely on its strong sales of high-margin trucks and commercial vans to support its broader business transformation.

As of Sept. 30, 2026, Ford's debt-to-equity ratio was approximately 4.48x. This metric, which is total debt divided by shareholder equity, shows the company relies heavily on borrowed funds to fuel its operations. The current ratio, which measures the ability to cover short-term liabilities with short-term assets, stands at approximately 1.1x. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was roughly $12.5 billion. This cash generation, defined as operating cash minus capital expenditures, helps provide the liquidity necessary to fund new vehicle platforms.

General Motors operates a diverse portfolio of iconic brands, including Chevrolet, Buick, GMC, and Cadillac, reaching customers in over 70 countries. The company focuses on maintaining high market share in the lucrative truck and SUV segments while simultaneously building out its Ultium battery platform. Beyond physical vehicles, the company leverages its OnStar connectivity service to create a digital ecosystem for both retail and fleet customers. This software-driven approach aims to improve customer retention and create new high-margin revenue streams through connected services.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $187.0 billion, a slight decrease of approximately 0.3% from the previous twelve months. Unlike its primary rival, the company remained profitable during this period, reporting net income of close to $4.0 billion. While revenue growth has been essentially flat, the ability to maintain positive net income demonstrates effective cost management across its traditional manufacturing base. This stability is vital as the company navigates the expensive transition toward a fully electric future.

As of Sept. 30, 2026, GM's debt-to-equity ratio was 2.0x. This ratio indicates the level of financial leverage the company uses, showing that its debt levels are lower relative to equity than those of its closest competitor. The current ratio is approximately 1.1x, indicating the firm has enough short-term assets to pay its upcoming bills. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was close to $11.1 billion.

Ford Motor faces significant operational risks, including potential supply chain disruptions for critical battery materials like lithium and cobalt. Quality control issues that lead to expensive recalls and warranty costs also remain a persistent concern for the manufacturer. Furthermore, the company must navigate a rapidly evolving electric vehicle market in China and increasing regulatory pressure regarding fuel economy standards. The capital-intensive nature of its current transformation plan means any significant slowdown in consumer demand could pressure its financial position.

Ford appears slightly more attractive on a sales basis, though both companies trade at remarkably similar earnings multiples relative to their future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with General Motors. It has turned its truck and SUV business into a dependable profit engine, and it is steadily bringing down the cost of its electric vehicle push.

Here's what tipped it for me: Its North American business, the heart of the company, is turning more of each sales dollar into operating profit year over year. GM also expects to generate well over $10 billion in cash this year, after covering its investments, and just approved a $20 billion share buyback, which gives each remaining share a bigger slice of the profits.

Ford has a strong hand in commercial trucks and vans, where its Pro business is the company's biggest profit maker. But Ford expects 2027 earnings to merely match this year's, and its electric vehicle unit is still losing billions of dollars. GM's EV losses, meanwhile, are falling, with more improvement expected next year. For long-term investors, the strong truck profits, a lighter EV burden, and a big buyback tip the scales toward GM for 2027.

#2974. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

#2972. Crypto Market Today, Nov. 22: Bitcoin Reclaims $60,000 After $1.8 Billion Liquidation Wipeout. Is the Worst Over?

Cryptocurrencies are attempting to stabilize following a brutal wave of forced liquidations, but the reopening of U.S. equity markets could determine whether the recovery gains momentum or quickly fades.

By RJ Fulton – Nov 22, 2026 at 5:20PM EST

Cryptocurrency markets showed signs of stabilization on Sunday, November 22, following a brutal sell-off that triggered approximately $1.8 billion in forced liquidations across digital asset markets. As of early evening, Bitcoin (CRYPTO: BTC) was up 1.15% to $60,785.36, while Ethereum (CRYPTO: ETH) gained 2.23% to $1,719.76. Solana (CRYPTO: SOL) lagged the broader market, edging 0.16% lower to $70.10. Despite the modest recovery, major cryptocurrencies remain under considerable pressure as investors assess whether the recent wave of forced selling has finally subsided.

The cryptocurrency market is attempting to stabilize following a sharp deterioration in global risk appetite that triggered widespread selling across equities, commodities, and digital assets. Friday's sell-off pushed Bitcoin to a two-year low near $55,000, while approximately $1.8 billion in leveraged cryptocurrency positions were liquidated as prices fell, forcing traders to unwind their exposure.

Recent selling pressure across cryptocurrencies coincided with a broader retreat from risk assets as concerns over elevated valuations, persistently high interest rates, and tightening financial conditions weighed on investor sentiment. The simultaneous weakness across markets suggests investors prioritized cash and liquidity over rotating capital into safer investments.

U.S.-listed spot cryptocurrency ETFs finished Friday's trading session with substantial losses. The iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (NASDAQ: IBIT) declined 5.87%, while the Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (NYSEMKT: FBTC) fell 6.16%. Meanwhile, the iShares Ethereum Trust ETF (NASDAQ: ETHA) plunged 9.16%.

These figures reflect Friday's closing prices, as U.S. stock exchanges were closed over the weekend. With Bitcoin and Ethereum recovering from their recent lows, Monday's reopening will provide the first opportunity for these ETFs to reflect the latest weekend price movements during regular trading.

The cryptocurrency market's modest recovery over the weekend offers some relief following last week's sharp sell-off, but it remains unclear whether the rebound reflects a meaningful improvement in investor demand or simply the exhaustion of forced selling. With leveraged positions liquidated during the downturn, the removal of excessive leverage could help stabilize prices. However, a sustainable recovery will require renewed buying interest rather than an absence of sellers.

The reopening of U.S. equity markets on Monday will provide an important signal for investors. Cryptocurrencies have been particularly sensitive to weakness in technology stocks, and another round of selling across risk assets could quickly undermine the weekend's gains. Investors should monitor whether Bitcoin can sustain its recovery above $60,000, Ethereum can regain $1,800, and Solana can establish support above $70.

#2963. Where Will USA Rare Earth Stock Be in 5 Years? This Mine Holds the Keys.

USA Rare Earth is building out its mine-to-magnet supply chain, and the Round Top mine in Texas will be an important part of its growth.

By Courtney Carlsen – Nov 22, 2026 at 5:30PM EST

Rare-earth elements are key ingredients in high-strength permanent magnets that enable many of today's modern electronics, clean energy technologies, and defense applications, including drones and precision-guided munitions.

The United States is taking steps to create a domestically controlled "mine-to-magnet" supply chain as it looks to reduce its dependence on China, which controls 70% of rare-earth extraction and 90% of processing, according to a research report from The Motley Fool.

The U.S. government is pouring money into rebuilding a rare-earth supply chain, and USA Rare Earth (USAR -8.73%) is one company playing a key role in unlocking America's rare-earth industry. The company made several acquisitions to hit the ground running. But over the next five years, developing the Round Top mine in Texas will be key to its long-term success.

In recent years, the U.S. has taken a pivotal role in expanding the country's rare-earth supply chains by providing funds, investing in companies in the space, setting price floors, and acting as a guarantor to break China's stronghold.

In June, the Department of Commerce finalized CHIPS Act funding of roughly $1.6 billion for USA Rare Earth, with $1.3 billion from senior secured loans and the remainder from grants.

The funding supports its efforts to enter rare-earth mining and processing, including its acquisitions of Less Common Metals and Serra Verde Group, as well as the expansion of processing and magnet-making facilities in Oklahoma and South Carolina.

In the coming years, USA Rare Earth will integrate its acquisitions, which help secure feedstock for rare-earth magnets while building out its U.S. manufacturing footprint. But a major part of its future lies in developing the Round Top deposit in West Texas.

The Round Top deposit is rich in heavy rare-earth elements (HREEs), which no U.S. mines currently produce at commercial scale. This would give USA Rare Earth a massive HREE supply, complementing its Serra Verde mine in Brazil, the largest-scale producer of HREEs outside Asia.

By owning the mine, factories, and processing, USA Rare Earth can capture margins across the entire value chain. The next five years are crucial for USA Rare Earth, and developing the Round Top project is a massive undertaking projected to cost around $1.4 billion. The company doesn't expect to begin commercial production at the Round Top until late 2028, with full-scale production coming by 2030.

For investors seeking exposure to the U.S. rare-earth supply chain build-out, USA Rare Earth is a highly speculative, risky stock, especially as it incurs gigantic costs to integrate and expand its mine-to-magnet supply chain over the next several years.

Cameron Carr of the Los Angeles Lakers drives to the basket. NBAE via Getty Images

During Friday's 106-104 home win over the Kings, rookie Cameron Carr got his most significant opportunity with the Lakers to date.

And his performance showed why he's deserving of more playing opportunities moving forward.

Cameron Carr of the Los Angeles Lakers speaks to the media. NBAE via Getty Images

Carr looked ready for the defensive physicality needed to hold his own on that end of the floor.

"That was huge," Quentin Grimes said. "I mean, as a rookie, you got to go out there and kind of earn minutes. Everybody kind of has to do that at one point some time in their career. Redick has said Carr and second-year forward Adou Thiero's development over the next couple of years could raise the team's collective ceiling.

But before they can be ceiling-raisers, Carr and Thiero need to establish a floor for themselves.

Jaden Hardy #7 and Cameron Carr #43 of the Los Angeles Lakers smile during a game against the Sacramento Kings. NBAE via Getty Images

With Reaves expected to make his return to the floor next week, playing time won't be available as it has been with him sidelined.

But Carr showed, at least for one game, he's ready to contribute more when his number is called.

And it's on the Lakers to find more chances for him so he can develop into the ceiling-raiser they're looking for him to be.

#2959. Apple Is Now 25% of Berkshire Hathaway's Stock Portfolio. Here's What Holding Every Share Tells Investors.

The iPhone maker still matters tremendously to the diversified Omaha company's finances.

By Neil Patel – Nov 22, 2026 at 5:35PM EST

Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -0.47%) (BRKB -0.36%), under the direction of Warren Buffett, began buying Apple (AAPL -1.90%) shares in the first quarter of 2016. That investment has worked out extremely well. At one point, the Cupertino tech giant accounted for 50% of the conglomerate's portfolio.

Even after trimming the position from fourth-quarter 2023 through the end of 2025, as of Nov. 17, the ”Magnificent Seven” stock represented 25% of Berkshire Hathaway's total public equity portfolio, and it is still its largest holding. The number of shares remained unchanged this year. This was despite there being a lot of shuffling among other positions.

Here's what Berkshire holding on to the Apple stake tells investors.

Over the three-month period from the start of July to the end of September, Apple shares traded at an average price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 36.8. Any rational investor would call this an expensive stock. That's doubly true for those who see equities through a value-focused lens. The share price has moved 29% higher in 2026.

Berkshire Hathaway didn't sell a single share during the third quarter. This might come as a surprise to those who have followed the diversified corporation's actions in recent years.

Warren Buffett oversaw the selling of Apple shares over multiple quarters from the end of 2023 to the end of 2025. For example, during the second quarter of 2024, when the technology stock traded at an average P/E multiple of 29, much lower than what it sells for today, Berkshire Hathaway cut its position dramatically.

While the single holding had become a significant part of the portfolio, it's fair to assume that valuation played a part in these decisions. Now, with a P/E ratio that's approaching 40, it looks like less emphasis is being placed on the valuation being an influential factor.

Greg Abel has been the chief executive officer of Berkshire Hathaway since the start of 2026. He has made notable moves in his short tenure thus far, but as outside observers, we have zero clue what he and Warren Buffett are discussing regarding the conglomerate's capital allocation strategy. All we can do is try to come up with some takeaways.

Staying true to Warren Buffett's investing philosophy, the Apple position is a clear signal that Berkshire Hathaway will hold on to its winners and let them run. "When we own portions of outstanding businesses with outstanding managements, our favorite holding period is forever," Buffett wrote in his 1988 shareholder letter.

The iPhone maker has been in the portfolio for nearly 11 years. Given how much of the market is driven by short-term behavior these days, that time horizon seems like an eternity.

If Berkshire Hathaway did sell some of its Apple shares in Q3, it wouldn't know where to move that capital. This is a persistent problem. Compared to the hundreds of billions of dollars on the balance sheet that earn low yields, an expensive Apple stock still offers the potential for higher returns. This benefits Berkshire Hathaway shareholders.

Valuation should always be viewed separately from the underlying business. In this instance, Berkshire Hathaway's inactivity is a vote of confidence in the quality of Apple's operations.

It's hard to dispute that the consumer tech juggernaut is one of the finest companies we've ever seen. A valuable brand, ongoing pricing power, customer loyalty, and innovative DNA are attractive traits. There's also its incredible financial performance, shown by a fiscal 2026 free cash flow margin of 28%.

This isn't a sign for the average investor to go out and buy Apple stock. It is proof, however, that buying great businesses and leaving them alone can work wonders for your portfolio.

#2953. Morningstar vs. Sezzle: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Sezzle is profitable and growing fast, but lending to shoppers carries credit and regulatory risk. Morningstar is a trusted research brand with rising profits, but its sales growth is modest.

By Sara Appino – Updated Nov 22, 2026 at 5:58PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Morningstar ›

Deciding between established financial research and high-growth payment technology requires a choice between steady market influence and rapid expansion. Is Morningstar (MORN -0.30%) or Sezzle (SEZL -6.09%) the better buy today?

Morningstar provides the critical research and data that power modern investment decisions for millions of retail and institutional users. Conversely, Sezzle is a disruptor in the payments space, offering flexible financing options for younger consumers. While both operate in the financial world, they cater to different market segments with unique risk and reward profiles.

Morningstar sells investment insights, data, and technology tools to a massive audience, including roughly 4.2 million individual investors and 425,000 financial advisors. The company serves eight primary customer groups, ranging from retirement plan providers and wealth managers to asset managers and fixed-income security issuers. Recently, it has integrated artificial intelligence through partnerships with major technology firms to automate analytics and improve user experience.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $2.6 billion, representing revenue growth of approximately 9.4%. This expansion helped the company achieve net income of close to $445.5 million during the same period. The steady climb in both top and bottom lines suggests consistent demand for the company's proprietary financial data and research services.

As of its September 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is roughly 1.8x (or 1.6x excluding operating lease liabilities). This ratio, which compares total debt to shareholder equity, reflects an increase in financial leverage after the company took on additional debt to fund acquisitions and share repurchases. The current ratio, which measures a company's ability to cover its short-term debts with short-term assets, is approximately 1.05x. In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, free cash flow reached nearly $492.8 million (following $442.6 million in fiscal year 2025).

Sezzle operates a platform that allows consumers to finance purchases at the point of sale. It focuses heavily on Gen Z and Millennial shoppers, connecting them with a vast network of merchants to increase average order values. By integrating with e-commerce platforms, the company captures a growing slice of the fintech stocks landscape.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached approximately $581.6 million, marking significant revenue growth of nearly 38.9%. This surge in activity led to a net income of close to $182.6 million for the period. The company also maintained a strong net margin of roughly 31.4% during this high-growth phase, indicating it is generating significant profit from its revenue.

As of its September 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio stands at approximately 4.2x, suggesting a robust ability to cover immediate liabilities. The debt-to-equity ratio is roughly 0.4x, meaning its total debt is less than half of its shareholder equity. In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, free cash flow reached nearly $297.6 million, providing the company with substantial capital to reinvest in its growth and fund share repurchases.

Morningstar faces risks tied to its reputation, as any perceived conflict of interest in its credit ratings or research could damage its brand. Its business is sensitive to market volatility, which can reduce asset-based fees when total market values decline. The company also competes with major data providers such as S&P Global (NYSE:SPGI) and MSCI (MSCI -2.22%) in the institutional research space.

Sezzle relies heavily on a single originating bank partner, WebBank, for the majority of its loans, which creates a significant concentration risk. It faces intense competition in the payment sector and ongoing regulatory scrutiny regarding consumer lending practices and interest rates. Additionally, antitrust litigation involving partners like Shopify (SHOP -6.25%) could indirectly impact its growth trajectory or existing merchant relationships.

Morningstar offers a more conservative entry point based on its sales, while Sezzle carries a higher premium that reflects its faster growth and superior net margin. The Forward P/E ratio compares a company's share price to its future earnings estimates, while the P/S ratio measures market value against sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

But there are some risks that give me pause: Sezzle lends to shoppers, so bad loans matter. Its newest products start with higher losses, and regulators and a lawsuit against Shopify add costs and uncertainty.

Morningstar, a trusted name in investing research, carries less credit risk, and its stock costs less relative to expected earnings. Its operating profit jumped last quarter, but sales growth is modest. Ultimately, I like that Sezzle is already profitable and still early in its expansion, with room to add new services. If you're investing for the long haul and can handle some risk, Sezzle's fast-growing payments platform looks like the better buy for 2027.

#2947. Upstart Is Yesterday's News. Is This Fintech Stock the Better Buy?

One of Upstart's competitors has a much stronger growth story and trades at a reasonable valuation.

By Thomas Niel – Nov 22, 2026 at 6:04PM EST

10 stocks we like better than SoFi Technologies ›

Upstart's (UPST -7.88%) days as a hot fintech stock have long since passed. It was trading near $400 per share back in 2021, but recently was changing hands for around $17 per share, a nearly 96% price decline.

While shares in this artificial intelligence (AI) lending technology company have seen brief partial rebounds in recent years, I’m looking more closely at fintech names with stronger growth stories, namely SoFi Technologies (SOFI -7.76%).

At first glance, it may seem illogical that Upstart continues to tumble. After all, following steep revenue declines in 2023 and 2024, sales and earnings have improved dramatically. In 2024, revenue and earnings per share (EPS) came in at $628.8 million and -$1.44, respectively. In 2025, revenue surged to just over $1 billion, with diluted EPS at $0.45 per share, marking a massive swing back to profitability.

For 2026, analysts' estimates call for Upstart to report $1.35 billion in sales and EPS of $1.84. Alas, there is a reason the market prices Upstart at such a low multiple to those estimates.

First, there is high uncertainty over future growth. Although the company recently reported a 34% increase in quarterly revenue to $371 million, and diluted earnings of $0.33 per share, up 43.4% from the prior year's quarter, management walked back its full-year guidance. Previously estimating  $1.4 billion in revenue and adjusted EBITDA of $294 million, Upstart's full-year 2026 outlook now calls for $1.32 billion in revenue and adjusted EBITDA of $260 million.

Second, the recent pullback in AI stocks and tech stocks has likely intensified the situation. This sell-off has spilled over into the fintech space, including shares in SoFi, a company more richly priced than Upstart but with far greater long-term upside potential.

Like Upstart, SoFi has recently reported strong growth. Last quarter, the digital-first financial services company reported net revenue of $1.4 billion, a 44% increase from the prior year's quarter. Adjusted EBITDA jumped 70% year over year, to $470 million. EPS came in at $0.18, a 63% increase from the $0.11 in diluted adjusted EPS reported for Q3 2025.

Better yet, SoFi's management provided bullish guidance for the coming year. In 2027, the company anticipates 27% adjusted net revenue growth and adjusted earnings of $0.90 per share, adding that it could achieve these goals without raising additional capital. With SoFi Technologies changing hands today at around $13.50 per share, the stock is trading at around 15 times next year's estimated earnings.

Longer-term, if SoFi can continue to grow at a moderate clip, steadily becoming a major "financial supermarket," shares could climb further, to past highs in the $30s per share, and beyond. Contrast that with Upstart, which, while still guiding for further growth, is revising that guidance downward. Worse yet, although both SoFi and Upstart are involved in the personal lending space, Upstart arguably has greater exposure. SoFi started as a lender but has since expanded into banking, brokerage, and other financial services, with its ability to cross-sell bolstering confidence in its growth story.

That said, it's still possible for Upstart to thrive while SoFi flounders. The sell-side remains divided on SoFi, perhaps reluctant to assign it a fintech valuation, given that it will eventually be classified as a bank stock. Upstart has tanked on poor guidance, but any sort of better-than-feared results and outlook could be enough to drive a relief rally. Nevertheless, if you're looking at either stock as a possible long-term investment rather than a trade, consider SoFi the better of the two.

#2941. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2940. Week 11 Game Recap: Bucs fall to 3-7; Behind coaching woes

Knee deep in the midst of one of the most surreal playoff races that imaginable, the Buccaneers would be looking to collect a win against the Detroit Lions in week 11. Can Bucky Irving, Baker Mayfield, and the Bucs' bodacious defense pull together to upset Dan Campbell's bunch on the road?

It was Baker Mayfield's ball first on Sunday and it was his chance to get sacked first— One play, three defenders getting off their blocks, and one sack of Baker Mayfield with a forced fumble to boot. The Bucs stayed in possession only to punt and play defense. Jared Goff's second pass and first completion of the day took Detroit across midfield, but two batted balls in a three play sequence forced the Lions into a long field attempt and long field goal miss.

The Buccaneers' offense found a few completions, a few third down conversions, and more importantly a bit of their stride. Tampa Bay moved nearly 50 yards to the Detroit 10-yard line before having to make a decision. Facing a fourth and one, Todd Bowles neglected a short field goal opportunity and chose to leave his offense on the field. Zac Robinson opted for Bucky Irving running into traffic and that traffic did more than slow him down, it stopped him. Tampa's failed fourth down attempt jump started Detroit's next drive.

50% of Tampa's star cornerback tandem would leave the field on Detroit's second drive and for Todd Bowles' defense, no Parrish = no good. The Lions tagged Benjamin Morrison for a big play and tagged him again for a touchdown. While Morrison did struggle to fill the Jacob Parrish's shoes, one of the early death nails for Tampa's defense though the early moments has been thorough inability to keep the Lions out of third down and one and a subsequent inability to stop Detroit from converting those third downs into first downs. 7-0, Lions.

Tampa's next start was kicked off by a false start— No doubt they had been holding that in, going an entire quarter without one. The Bucs' next drive no longer featured third down as an unconquerable foe third downs— First down Cade Otton, first down Chris Godwin, however, old habits die hard— All out blitz, sack, punt.

With the seems as open as a 7/11 in New York City, Jared Goff picked apart Tampa Bay's zones all the way into the final two minutes of the half and field goal range. 10-0, Lions.

Rocking a bad thumb, a bad foot, and a bad offense Baker Mayfield had a 1:30 from the 35-yard line to cut into the Lions' lead. One more big scramble from the Buccaneers' injured quarterback and rare down the field throw to Bucky Irving planted Tampa Bay squarely in Chase McLaughlin's range. The Buccaneers did not choose to settle, they took a shot at the end zone, trying for six. Baker Mayfield reared back and lofted a beautiful drop into the bucket to Ted Hurst III for a 23-yard touchdown strike. Hurst III's second grab of the drive gave the Bucs' a feeling of what could be, in a season where deep passes or a passing game with any consistent at all has been a needle in hay stack, Ted Hurst III's score before half flashed a glimmer of what it could be if it all ever clicked. 10-7, Detroit.

Following a Detroit punt, the Buccaneers' offense had the chance to capitalize on their end of half success— They did not. Their drive began with an 80-yard touchdown scamper by Bucky Irving (called back for a Luke Goedeke hold) and ended with a punt.

After coming mere moments away from holding a lead over the Detroit Lions, the Bucs were back on defense and still trailing. Once again, no Parrish = no good. Jameson Williams ran by Benjamin Morrison like Ferrari passing a Hyundai at a stop light. Williams' score gave the Lions their largest lead of the day (10 points)— 17-7, home town team.

8:56 left in the third, down 10, plenty of time to rally— Also plenty of time for a close game to become a rout. In a pinch, the Buccaneers' offense turned to their backs. Bucky Irving on the ground and Kenny Gainwell through the air maneuvered Zac Robinson's offense down the field and into the end zone for a second touchdown of the day— Bucky Irving's score brought the Buccaneers back to whin three. 17-14, team awkwardly dressed like a Carolina Panthers spin off.

The game's final 15 minutes were Detroit's to start. Tampa's defense notched their first sack of the day in order to force Detroit into a punt. Pinned deep at the nine, the Bucs needed some Mayfield magic to flip the scoreboard— Somebody might've miscalculated the formula on the magic because the Buccaneers finished their drive with a punt, a bad punt at that. Easy does it for the Lions' offense, smooth sailing into the end zone for another touchdown— 24-14, Lions.

With 5:24 remaining in the game, the Bucs found Emeka Egbuka to pick up a first down. That would be the only first down they would earn because Tampa Bay's offense (in 2026) is to sacks like sand is to a beach. Tampa Bay's turnover on downs segued into a nice undisciplined brawl (discipline falls on the coach) headed by Vita Vea and the Buccaneer's weekly collapse was complete— Seven more points for Jahmyr Gibbs, 31-14, not Todd Bowles' team.

A hurt, beat down Baker Mayfield rallied the troops for one last gasp of air. Just under two minutes to go and the Bucs road penalty flags down to the red zone and Ted Hurst III across the goal line. 31-21, Detroit.
