#2834. Social Security benefits are rising in 2027, but other changes could affect what you keep

Whether you're working or retired, changes to Social Security in 2027 could affect your finances.

Starting in January, millions of program beneficiaries will get a 3.6% increase to their benefits, thanks to the annual cost-of-living adjustment. But rising Medicare premiums could chip away at the increase — and as beneficiaries' incomes rise, that may affect the taxes they owe and their eligibility for a new senior deduction.

Meanwhile, a higher taxable earnings cap means some workers will pay Social Security taxes on more income, reducing their paycheck.

Here's a breakdown of what to expect in the new year.

Social Security's limit for payroll taxes is adjusted every year to reflect the average wage index.

In 2027, the taxable maximum will go up to $192,300 in earnings, up from $184,500 in 2026. Workers and employers each pay a 6.2% payroll tax on earnings up to that limit — or 12.4% for self-employed workers. Medicare taxes of 1.45% for both workers and employers are not subject to a limit.

In 2027, workers who earn $192,300 or more will pay $11,922.60 in total Social Security payroll taxes, according to the Social Security Administration, while self-employed workers will pay $23,845.20.

"If you're somebody whose income is over what the new threshold is going to be, this increase in the threshold results in a tax increase to you," said Tim Steffen, director of advanced planning at financial services firm Baird.

The 3.6% Social Security cost-of-living adjustment for 2027 is slated to increase retirement benefits by about $75 per month on average, according to the Social Security Administration.

The annual adjustment is the largest boost to benefits since 2023 due to rising inflation. The COLA averaged 2.6% from 2000 to 2026, according to the Senior Citizens League, a nonpartisan senior group.

Around 72 million Social Security beneficiaries will see benefit increases, according to the agency.

The maximum benefit for a worker at full retirement age — when individuals become eligible for 100% of the benefits they've earned — will rise to $4,188 per month in 2027, up from $4,152 per month in 2026, according to the federal agency. To be eligible for the maximum benefit at full retirement age — typically age 66 to 67 — you generally have to earn up to the taxable threshold for each of your working years factored into the calculation.

How much of the COLA increase Social Security beneficiaries see depends in part on their Medicare premiums, which may be deducted directly from benefit checks.

Monthly rates are poised to increase for both Medicare Part B, which covers medically necessary services and preventive care, and Part D, which provides prescription drug coverage.

The standard monthly Medicare Part B premium will be $209.40 in 2027 — a $6.50 increase from $202.90 in 2026, according to the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services.

"It's not often that we see a Part B [premium] increase that is lower than the COLA," Mary Johnson, an independent Social Security and Medicare policy analyst, said of the 3.2% increase. The last time that happened was in 2023, she said.

Medicare Part D beneficiaries may also pay higher premiums in 2027.

Assuming enrollees keep the same plan, the average enrollment-weighted monthly Part D premium may be $51 in 2027, up 50% from $34 in 2026, according to projections from KFF, a provider of health policy research and polling. That is higher than CMS' projections, which forecast the national average premium for standalone Part D plans will rise about $1, to $36, in 2027 from $35 in 2026.

Standard monthly premiums for Part B and plan-specific premiums for Part D apply to individuals who have $112,000 or less in modified adjusted gross income, or $224,000 for married couples. Beneficiaries with higher income pay larger premiums because of income-related monthly adjustment amounts, which affect 9% of Medicare Part B and Part D beneficiaries, according to CMS.

With bigger Social Security benefits, more households may find that some portion of benefits is taxable — though a new senior deduction may help provide some relief to those who are eligible.

Social Security benefits may be subject to federal income taxes for certain individuals. The thresholds at which those taxes are applied are not adjusted from year to year.

Whether or not benefits are taxed is determined by a formula known as combined income — the sum of adjusted gross income, tax-exempt interest income and half of Social Security benefits:

Individuals who are subject to taxes may opt to withhold taxes from their Social Security benefit checks, rather than face a potential bill during tax filing season.

Separately, a temporary senior deduction of up to $6,000 for individuals ages 65 and over may help to indirectly reduce taxes on Social Security benefits.

That deduction, part of the One Big Beautiful Bill Act in 2025, helps reduce retirees' taxable income, according to Steffen. It's in place through 2028. Because the break phases out for individuals with modified adjusted gross income starting at $75,000 — and for married couples with $150,000 — it's important to watch your income if you're close to those limits, Steffen said.

"Keep in mind that if you're over 65, you might qualify for this deduction," Steffen said. "But if you let your income get too big, you might lose the deduction."

Moreover, any extra income that causes you to lose the deduction may also come with its own taxes, making it more expensive, according to Steffen.

"What we've been telling our clients is that tax planning is a lot more complicated than it used to be, and you can't do it on the back of an envelope anymore," he said. "You need to use good tools that can do good calculations for you."

Individuals who claim Social Security retirement benefits before their full retirement age and who continue to work may be subject to withholdings.

In 2027, individuals under full retirement age may earn up to $25,560 per year, up from $24,480 in 2026, without adjustments to their Social Security benefits. For every $2 in earnings above that limit, $1 in Social Security benefits will be withheld.

Importantly, those withholdings are not lost completely. Once a beneficiary reaches full retirement age, the withheld money will be credited to their benefits over their remaining lifespan.

In the year individuals reach full retirement age, the retirement earnings test limit is higher — $67,920 per year in 2027, up from $65,160 per year in 2026. For every $3 in earnings above that limit, $1 in Social Security benefits is withheld.

Starting the month a beneficiary reaches full retirement age, no earnings are withheld.

#2833. Aaron Rodgers, DK Metcalf can't fix Steelers' broken passing game after Eagles loss

The Pittsburgh Steelers had another ugly day offensively and dropped a 19-9 decision to the Philadelphia Eagles on Nov. 22 at Lincoln Financial Field.

Top receiver DK Metcalf was ejected after multiple personal-foul penalties as the passing game struggled. Quarterback Aaron Rodgers finished the day with 30 completions on 40 attempts for 227 yards and a touchdown.

On the other side, the defense played fairly well, but not well enough to cope with the lack of scoring from the offense.

Here's USA TODAY Sports' report card on the action from a Pittsburgh perspective.

Rodgers simply isn't a threat to beat defenses downfield consistently right now. He was able to get some momentum going at times with the short and quick passes that have proven effective for the Steelers at a few different points this season. It's also true that his receivers need to be open consistently for him to hit some of the more explosive plays. They frequently are not. None of that changes the reality that the lack of a deeper passing game is a major problem for this offense right now, and a near 43-year-old quarterback needs to share responsibility for the failures with everyone else, whether his career is likely to end at the Pro Football Hall of Fame or not.

For the second game in a row, Jaylen Warren wasn't able to make practically anyone miss. The blocking wasn't great, but Warren frequently hasn't needed it to be to transcend and make a lot of explosive plays this season. With left guard Mason McCormick out, possibly for a while, the sledding isn't likely to get easier. The Steelers need more elusiveness from him and primary backup Rico Dowdle. Warren had 28 yards on nine carries. Dowdle had 15 on five touches.

Roman Wilson's return to the lineup didn't lead to much meaningful direct production, nor did it seem to make life easier for top receivers DK Metcalf and Germie Bernard. The former was frequently invisible, as he was in the first half of the loss to Cincinnati last week, and he got himself ejected with a pair of senseless personal-foul penalties. His numbers continue to track toward career lows and the conduct is only compounding the headache he's becoming. Bernard made some of those plays in the short and quick range, which was an encouraging sign after he was held off the scoresheet by the Bengals. Practice squad veteran Max Hurleman added his first NFL touchdown late. It wasn't a performance that engendered much confidence in the group as a whole, though.

Pat Freiermuth was more active than he has been recently as a safety valve for Rodgers, but the downfield success still was not there. Darnell Washington was also relatively quiet. As the receivers continue to struggle, you'd like to see more from these two. They're both playing on lengthy contract extensions to make more plays to jump-start this struggling offense.

There was no push ahead of Dowdle and Warren in the running game. In the passing game, Rodgers was scrambling to avoid pressure frequently and was sacked five times. Much of the momentum this group seemed to establish before the bye has dissipated. The loss of McCormick is certainly disruptive, so there's a temptation to give these guys some benefit of the doubt as they figure life out without him. The results need to be better even with that caveat, though, particularly in the running game. This offense will quickly become one dimensional if this group can't figure it out.

Saquon Barkley's 38-yard touchdown run was a game changer that this group was primarily responsible for. Cam Heyward and Co. did well to contain him otherwise - he had just 37 yards beyond that one big play - and were frequently in the backfield making plays. With the offense playing the way it was, the team just needed them to be better against a struggling offense like Philadelphia's.

Patrick Queen was all over the ball yet again. He's playing some of the best football of his life within DC Patrick Graham's defense right now. Alex Highsmith forced a fumble and generated two long-awaited sacks before an injury disrupted his day. T.J. Watt was once again mostly invisible, except while he was beaten badly on the back side of the long Barkley touchdown run.

Eagles quarterback Joe Milton had chances to make the Steelers pay but missed with some poorly timed overthrows into solid coverage. Beyond a 38-yard completion to Dallas Goedert, the Pittsburgh defensive backs avoided most of the explosive plays that cost them dearly against the Bengals. That was a positive step forward for this soft zone scheme.

McCarthy does not seem to have control over whatever is going on with Metcalf this season. For as much criticism as Mike Tomlin took for being too much of a "player's coach," it's fair to question what's going on in McCarthy's locker room when his top receiver not only can't produce, but also can't stay on the field because of his temper. The lack of downfield passing is also a reflection of his playcalling.

#2827. Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, ‘miserable' weeks

Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, 'miserable' weeks originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Dominick Barlow is on the verge of his season debut with the Sixers.

The team listed Barlow as probable for its Saturday afternoon matchup with the Bucks at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Barlow practiced Friday and has been making clear progress in his recent on-court work recovering from an injury formally classified as a "lower back stress reaction."

He was a reliable rotation fixture last season, playing 71 regular-season games and starting 59. Barlow posted 7.7 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.2 assists and 0.9 steals per contest in 23.8 minutes per game. The Sixers then made a common-sense call to exercise his $3.4 million option for the 2026-27 season.

The 23-year-old described his back injury as "an overuse thing."

"I guess there was an area or maybe I started my (offseason) workouts too early," he said. "I don't know what it was fully in terms of what caused it. Once it happened, it happened. Nothing I can do about it besides try to correct it and get my body feeling as good as possible."

He called the past few weeks "miserable" as he rehabbed and waited on the green light by the Sixers' training staff.

"The days just felt longer," Barlow said. "Trying to be engaged mentally, which I think I've done a good job with, but the physical aspect wasn't there. I'm somebody that's prided himself on competing, being available. Not being able to do that was hard, but I'm excited to kind of put this past me."

With LeBron James and Jaylen Brown on the team now, Barlow won't start at forward when the Sixers are at full strength.

He's still a multi-dimensional frontcourt piece for Sixers head coach Nick Nurse who may very well get substantial opportunities at backup center.

"Just cutting, playing hard, being ready to make the open shot," Barlow said. "And then I don't think you can ever have too much defensive versatility. I think that's something I can bring, try to help us be more versatile."

When Barlow checks into his first game of the season, he'll appreciate the moment.

"You don't really realize what you have until it's not there (anymore)," he said. "I'm just excited to play. …. Just playing the first stint … that's going to be the main thing I'm looking forward to."

A Sixers official said the following players were all "unrestricted" at the team's light practice:

Dean Wade (left hamstring soreness/tightness)

Bona still needs to practice with contact before a return is on the table, according to the official. Saturday's game will mark his eighth consecutive absence.

Brown and Wade have both been ruled out for the 13-3 Sixers' meeting with the 1-15 Bucks. Maxey was listed as questionable Friday night.

#2825. ‘More than just a game’: How GTA VI’s blockbuster launch fuels a booming content economy

Grand Theft Auto VI has exploded out of the gate after a 13-year wait, drawing millions of buyers while fueling a parallel economy of livestreams, clips and other content built around people watching the game as much as playing it.

GTA VI has delivered a blockbuster debut, with about 19.1 million copies sold — including preorders — by Thursday morning, representing roughly $1.84 billion in sales, according to Sensor Tower. The GTA franchise itself has sold more than 470 million units worldwide.

Take-Two Interactive, which owns the studio behind the game, has not yet disclosed a profit figure for the title.

Ross Gerber, president and CEO of Gerber Kawasaki Wealth & Investment Management, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" that he sees further upside for Take-Two, which his firm holds in its portfolio.

"It's crazy good. I've never seen anything like it," Gerber said, hailing the title as "more than just a game" and pointing to the franchise's potential to expand into film and television.

But that launch is also spawning a second economy around the enormous global hype and demand for GTA VI content.

Viewers watched 125.5 million hours of the title's content in its first 24 hours across platforms including Twitch, YouTube and Kick, according to Streams Charts.

YouTube creator Darren "IShowSpeed" Watkins Jr. led streamers with 4.47 million hours viewed across YouTube and Twitch. Separately, Twitch tracker CastAccord recorded about two million hours watched over roughly 36 hours of streamer Kai Cenat's launch broadcast.

Both creators had built anticipation with trailers for their own GTA VI coverage. As of Nov. 19, IShowSpeed's trailer had drawn 3.7 million YouTube views, while Cenat's had amassed 2.7 million.

According to Forbes, IShowSpeed and Cenat are estimated to earn about $30 million and $8.5 million a year, respectively, from their broader creator businesses.

Rockstar Games, the studio behind the franchise, has also moved to capitalize on the surge in viewer interest, partnering with YouTube on a 24-hour launch broadcast and introducing themed gifts that viewers could send during select streams.

Outside creators and agencies are also trying to capture the surge in demand for Grand Theft Auto content.

Agency Clipped & Shipped is offering a $10,000 prize pool to editors who can drive audiences to clips from Kai Cenat's streams, with qualifying submissions distributed across YouTube Shorts, TikTok, Instagram and X.

A separate campaign offered $1 per 1,000 views for clips of streamer KreekCraft, with individual submissions eligible to earn up to $400.

Those audiences could translate into thousands of dollars in creator revenue.

Using AIR Media-Tech's U.S. YouTube Shorts benchmark, 1.5 million eligible engaged views could generate about $490, while 5.1 million would yield roughly $1,670.

The two Vice City Dispatch videos could generate about $3,840 based on higher benchmark rates for long-form content. The channels' actual earnings could not be independently verified.

Moreover, the content economy is becoming increasingly automated.

On X, users are pitching artificial intelligence-assisted clipping operations that scan livestreams for spikes in chat activity, automatically cut highlights and distribute them across multiple accounts.

One user claimed an operation generated $21,000 overnight from 2.7 million views, equivalent to about $7.78 per 1,000 reported views.

The earnings claims could not be independently verified. X bases payouts on qualified impressions from Premium users, which are not reflected in public view counts.

And appetite for more content — and more revenue — may only be starting.

Wedbush analyst Alicia Reese told CNBC that in-game purchases could generate "years of high-margin recurring revenue" for Take-Two, which she believes investors have yet to fully price in.

She expects the console launch to be followed by a PC release and then an online multiplayer mode, mirroring the rollout of the previous title in the franchise.

Rockstar's launch announcement listed PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X and S, with no timetable for either potential follow-on. GTA VI has also picked up eight nominations for the Game Awards, including game of the year, best game direction, narrative, art direction, score and music, audio design, action/adventure and performance.

#2823. The Chiefs took care of business in soft three-game stretch

It's time for the Kansas City Chiefs to turn the page to a perennially important matchup with the AFC's No. 1 seed, the Buffalo Bills, on Thanksgiving night. It's the first of three road games against teams with winning records (the Los Angeles Rams on the following Thursday before squaring off with the Cincinnati Bengals).

The anticipation for this game has built since the Chiefs' last-second loss to the Denver Broncos in Week 8, which sent Kansas City to a 5-2 record. Since then, the softest three-game stretch of the team's schedule provided a chance to ramp up before squaring off with Buffalo.

In that time, the Chiefs seldom played with their food, keeping their foot on the gas pedal against the New York Jets, Atlanta Falcons, and Arizona Cardinals for a combined 104-32 score. Backup quarterback Justin Fields took the final snaps for the offense in all three games, including almost the entirety of the fourth quarter against the Cardinals.

It's exactly what the team needed ahead of the rest of the schedule, seemingly filled with playoff contenders.

The Chief took care of business in style, too. The team has averaged 422 total yards over the last four weeks, including the loss to Denver, a rate that would lead the NFL for the season by more than 20 yards.

Quarterback Patrick Mahomes has also been dominant in this stretch, rarely losing rhythm with the Chiefs' offense since the debacle against the Seattle Seahawks. He has not thrown an interception since that game nearly a month ago.

There was only so much Kansas City could prove about its odds of winning the Super Bowl this year in the last three wins, but the team did seemingly all anyone in Chiefs Kingdom could ask for: dominate bad teams thoroughly.

Now, the Chiefs need to take the next step and find a way to win crucial road games, especially against conference foes vying for the No. 1 seed like Buffalo and Cincinnati.

#2822. S&P 500 correction: Five forces behind the market sell-off

Esteemed Wharton professor Jeremy Siegel was more animated than usual on Friday afternoon... with good reason.

The normally staid author of "Stocks for the Long Run" spoke to CNBC on Friday as the market careened toward some ugly milestones. The S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average closed in correction territory, off more than 10% from their records. The Nasdaq Composite lost its gain for the year and the more concentrated Nasdaq-100 entered bear market territory on an intraday basis.

"These things are like straws that break the camel's back. You know what I mean? Everyone [is] all, 'I don't care, I don't care cause everything else is good' and when those straws keep on going... Bang," said Siegel.

Just a month-and-a-half ago, the S&P 500 and the Nasdaq Composite were at record highs. But the straws were already adding up by then and a market in denial is just now recognizing them.

Here are the reasons behind the sell-off:

The breaking point came this week in the form of the initial public offering of artificial intelligence leader Anthropic and a pick-up in the selling intensity for one-time AI chip leader Nvidia.

Anthropic upped its offering amount a day before going public despite the already dimming climate around AI-related stocks. The move ensured it would be the biggest IPO ever, but played into the fears that this debut — following SpaceX's monster offering over the summer — was a collective cash-out moment for the AI complex that will leave the investing masses holding the bag.

In other words, it could be the ultimate "sell the news" moment for the AI trade.

On Thursday, Anthropic traded from its $83 IPO price to a high above $115 before instantly rolling over and taking the AI trade with it. Anthropic's sell-off intensified Friday, and when the dust settled Anthropic closed the week at $88.91.

The Nasdaq posted its worst week since the Covid plunge of March 2020, down 10%.

Nvidia fell 14% on Friday with the selling intensifying after a Reuters headline hit the tape that the chipmaker's accounting is the focus of a Securities and Exchange Commission probe.

Nvidia is now down nearly 40% in November alone, a month that saw the AI bull market's leading salesman, Jensen Huang, resign as its CEO because of an affair with an alleged Chinese spy that raised speculation about whether the company's chips were ending up in Beijing's hands despite government restrictions.

To start the week, the Commerce Department tightened restrictions on U.S.-made chips to stop China from accessing them remotely from other countries.

A solid earnings report on Tuesday fell flat as Nvidia's board definitively ruled out any chance Huang would return, disappointing some of the stock's fanatics still holding out hope for his far-fetched return. Nvidia ended the week with a 23% loss.

The S&P 500 traded below its 200-day moving average — what's considered a measure of a security's long-term trend — on Tuesday and made a convincing break of it on Thursday. This turned many on Wall Street— like commodity trading advisors — into automatic sellers on the market. So-called CTAs are individuals or organizations that advise others on trading futures and swaps across a range of assets.

"We know there's a lot of trend riders on these markets," said Siegel. "They have their trend lines. They've ridden it up and as soon as it goes down, they're selling and they're selling and they're selling and they just perpetuate" the decline, he said.

The Iran conflict has caused the price oil to surge and while it's off its highs it still remains above $83 a barrel, while the fees for transiting the Strait of Hormuz and the rest of the region keep persistent pressure on global prices.

A series of numbers earlier in the month showed inflation is still far from the Federal Reserve's 2% target, sparking a run of central bank officials to warn they may have to get more aggressive with interest rates.

After increasing rates in September by a quarter point, the Fed skipped a hike in late October and sparked concern among bond investors it may be falling behind the inflation fight. The 10-year Treasury yield subsequently jumped above 5% and stayed there until a flight of safe haven fixed income buying brought it back below that level on Friday.

Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller said this week that the Fed may need to do a half percentage point hike next month to catch up.

"I'm very, very concerned about inflation. It's kind of headed in the wrong direction, and it's spreading out," Waller told CNBC.

"Don't fight the Fed" has long been a maxim on Wall Street and right now expectations are that the central bank will need to raise rates in December and again in January.

And late-breaking on Friday was a report from the Financial Times that the Trump Administration is considering restarting strikes on Iran.

Before the grim market milestones were met on Friday, it had long lost its mojo on fears that the pace of growth driven by AI companies was slowing.

As Anthropic prepared to go public, its third-quarter figures released earlier this month showed revenue growth last quarter was 47%, down from 144% in the second quarter.

AI momentum trades, like the VanEck Semiconductor ETF and the iShares MSCI USA Momentum Factor ETF, all topped out during the summer.

The fears of slowing growth picked up this month after the Democrats won control of Congress and key governorships, fueling speculation that states — or even the whole country — could implement stricter rules on data center construction.

CoreWeave, which builds data centers using high amounts of debt, is down 71% from its high. Caterpillar, which has reinvented itself as an AI play because of data center construction and generators, is down more than 50% from its high.

The view of Siegel and many market observers is that the economy remains in good enough shape that this will just be a run-of-the-mill correction that resets a bull market that had raised people's expectations too high because of its early ferocity.

The S&P 500 returned more than 20% in 2023 and 2024 and then gained 16% last year.

"The average over long periods for the stock market is 8%, 9% a year up," said Siegel. "People began to think that 15 to 20 [percent] was going to be the norm with AI and we have to bring that down to reality."

The only problem with that theory is that the stock market and its wealth effect was one of the major factors underpinning the economy. Stocks are a greater source of household wealth — especially for baby boomers — than they ever have been before.

With the S&P 500 clinging to a 1% gain for the year, this stock market rout could end up tipping off a virtuous cycle that slows the economy, and in turn, leads to bigger equity losses.

#2821. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2819. This Anthropic proxy play is getting traders' attention – here's where they see it going

Who says you can't tailgate an IPO?Traders are preparing for this week's blockbuster Anthropic IPO in different ways, including buying options on one publicly-traded fund that invests in private assets.The Destiny Tech100 (DXYZ) is a publicly-traded, closed-end fund that invests in equity, equity-linked instruments, or shares of private funds and venture companies to gain exposure to private companies. Currently, 14% of the fund's assets are tied to Anthropic, according to the company's website.Options volume on the fund jumped to more than 10x the 30-day average Monday, according to Cboe LiveVol data. Of the 42,300 contracts traded by midday, 41,800 were calls, with just 141 puts likely opened by buyers.

Just shy of $5.5 million in options premium on DXYZ traded on Monday, with almost all of it tied to calls, but just marginally more spent buying than selling calls, according to SpotGamma. The highest-volume contract is currently the 30-strike call expiring Friday, a 90-cent contract that needs the fund to rally 19% into the weekend to pay off.Traders may want to be wary of how the fund traded around the SpaceX IPO: DXYZ surged almost 150% in May before unwinding all the gains in the weeks ahead of SpaceX's debut. Since then, the fund has barely moved, trading around $26.There are other bullish signs surrounding Anthropic's IPO in the derivatives markets. Current prices for perpetual futures on the blockchain exchange Hyperliquid are implying an opening price of $98, a big pop from the $70-$78 price range quoted in the company's amended S1 filing. The AI giant is expected to price Wednesday evening and begin trading on the Nasdaq on Thursday under the ticker ANT.

#2817. Cowboys vs. Titans: 5 winners and 3 losers from the game

The Dallas Cowboys entered Sunday knowing there was very little margin left for error in their season.

Against the Tennessee Titans, the offense responded in emphatic fashion. Dallas moved the football almost at will and built a commanding advantage, although some familiar discipline and defensive problems prevented the performance from being completely clean.

Either way, the Cowboys get the home victory winning 51-30. Here are today's winners and losers from the win at home.

Dak Prescott completely controlled this game. Prescott went 28-of-33 for 302 yards and four touchdown passes, while also adding a rushing touchdown himself. He also connected on a franchise-record 19 consecutive passes. He has found answers at every level of the field and, most importantly, hasn't given Tennessee anything through turnovers.

The efficiency was absolutely outstanding. Prescott connected with CeeDee Lamb, George Pickens and Javonte Williams for touchdowns and repeatedly punished Tennessee when it tried to bring pressure or crowd the line. A testament to his control in this game, Prescott hit eight different receivers in this game and rushed three times for 10 yards.

Javonte Williams hasn't produced a massive rushing total, but he keeps finding the end zone. Williams scored Dallas' opening touchdown from one yard before later catching an 11-yard touchdown pass from Prescott in the third quarter.

That gives Dallas another example of why Williams has become such an important part of this offense. He's capable of doing the dirty work around the goal line while also contributing as a receiver when defenses focus too heavily on Lamb and Pickens. Plus he still provides quality pass protection.

This was a great example of just how difficult the Cowboys receiver room can be to defend when everybody contributes.

It starts with CeeDee Lamb, who produced another monster afternoon with 11 catches for 132 yards and a touchdown. Tennessee knew where the football was going and still couldn't stop him. Lamb consistently moved the chains, produced yards after the catch and scored from eight yards out in the second quarter.

Then there was George Pickens, who gave Dallas the complementary threat it needs opposite Lamb. Pickens reached seven receptions for 102 yards and a touchdown, averaging 14.6 yards per catch. His 10-yard touchdown in the second quarter helped Dallas build a 27-6 advantage. He also added a six-yard rushing attempt, showing how Dallas continues finding different ways to get the football into his hands.

And then there's KaVontae Turpin. His contribution didn't come through the traditional passing game, but he's still part of what makes this receiver room so difficult to defend. Dallas gave Turpin two designed rushing attempts and he turned them into 41 yards and a touchdown, including a 22-yard score around the left side.

James Houston made the most of his opportunities. His biggest play came late in the first quarter when he exploded off the edge and dropped Cam Ward for an eight-yard sack on third down, immediately ending Tennessee's possession. Houston had already created pressure on the previous snap and consistently looked disruptive when Dallas allowed him to attack.

He actually got to Ward again during the second quarter for what would have been a 13-yard sack, only for a Joey Porter Jr. facemask penalty to wipe the play away. The sack didn't count statistically, but the rep still showed Houston beating his blocker and finishing at the quarterback. He finally got that sack back later in the game.

Houston keeps making the argument that he deserves more opportunities to provide it, and a day with two sacks that could have been three is a good starting point for that argument.

Quinnen Williams continues to look like the centerpiece Dallas hoped it was acquiring. Williams recorded a sack of Cam Ward in the second quarter, getting through the interior to drop him for a loss. He also produced one of his best run-defense snaps of the afternoon by exploding into the backfield and dropping former Cowboy Tony Pollard for a two-yard loss.

Williams also affected the passing game without always appearing in the sack column, including forcing an incompletion on third down later in the second quarter. He also tipped to balls at the line of scrimmage.

The high point of what he did today, however, was the great strip-sack late in the game. Other than an interception, Williams marked off every major defensive statistical category today, and no one does that by accident.

Mike Pettine's first game calling the defense has produced some encouraging moments. The explosive-play problem hasn't disappeared.

The clearest example came in the third quarter when rookie Carnell Tate got loose for a 59-yard touchdown, immediately answering a Dallas score and allowing Tennessee to remain within striking distance.

The Cowboys can produce several excellent defensive snaps, generate pressure and force an opponent behind schedule, only for a lapse in concentration to wipe all of it away. That has been the story of this defense throughout the season.

This is the part of Dallas' performance that Brian Schottenheimer will hate when he watches the film. The Cowboys committed 11 penalties for 128 yards, that's far too much free yardage against anyone, never mind for a team that has spent most of the season fighting its own discipline problems.

George Pickens' third-quarter taunting penalty was particularly unnecessary. Prescott had just scored to extend Dallas' advantage, yet Pickens immediately gave away 15 yards after the play.

Dallas has been good enough offensively to overcome the penalties today, but a different team on a different day this is bad.

The Cowboys defense wasn't suddenly dominant against Tennessee, but it looked different this week, and that isn't a particularly good look for Christian Parker.

Tennessee still created explosive plays and scored points, but their touchdowns during the meat of the game came from a long pass interference penalty, and the failure to touch Tate down on what should have been a medium gain.

What stood out, though, was how much more unified the unit appeared. Communication looked cleaner, players seemed more certain of their assignments and there appeared to be less confusion before the snap.

Dallas also generated pressure at important moments and generally looked more organized despite Pettine only recently joining the staff. If the defense immediately looks calmer and more connected in the very first game after somebody else takes over the play-calling, questions are naturally going to be asked about what was happening before.

Pettine hasn't fixed the Cowboys defense in one afternoon, but even a noticeable step toward looking like a coherent unit is a huge jump from what was happening.

#2816. Trump says there could be a 'positive spin' from the latest FBI database hack

President Donald Trump suggested Saturday that there could be a bright spot in the latest incursion into the FBI computer database.

The president confirmed in an interview with Fox News that a system has been breached that houses information used by law enforcement at all levels to share criminal justice information.

The hackers accessed the National Criminal Information Center (NCIC), a database that includes FBI fingerprint records, wanted persons, violent felons, missing persons and related information, two people familiar with the matter told MS NOW. The database is restricted to law enforcement use but not classified and it is unclear what data was extracted.

CNN had first reported on the suspected hack, citing sources familiar with the issue.

In a wide-ranging interview on Fox, Trump said there could be upside from the news.

"Maybe we'll learn about the voter fraud, all the voter fraud that took place," he said. "I always like to put things on a positive spin."

This would be the second recent intrusion into the FBI system; the previous one came in September, when a group known as "ShinyHunters" broke into an FBI jobs website. The result compromised data on current and former FBI employees and shut down part of the agency's operations until the issue was resolved.

Trump said it's possible there could be information gleaned from the latest hack.

"I wonder who's doing it, because wouldn't it be great to learn about Russia, Russia, Russia?" he said. "People should hear about it, because it actually put our country in danger, and so maybe we'll learn things that we wouldn't have learned otherwise."

The FBI did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

#2814. ‘It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround

'It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers created their two cleanest late-game looks several hours before they needed them.

During Wednesday morning's shootaround in Orlando, Nick Nurse had the Sixers work on offensive material they had not consistently used this season. They tested one of those actions during the first half, returned to it throughout the closing minutes and watched it produce the shots they needed to complete a 17-point comeback.

"We worked on some stuff this morning that we hadn't used before, and they ran all that stuff very, very well and very calmly and with composure," Nurse told reporters.

The Sixers have earned six of their 13 wins after trailing by double digits. They are also 11-0 in games that reached clutch time.

Relying on comebacks is not a sustainable plan. The repeated late-game situations, however, have forced a new roster to respond to different lineups, coverages and responsibilities while the season is still young.

Each close finish gives Nurse more information about which players and actions hold up when possessions tighten. Wednesday gave him a new option.

Anfernee Simons' clinching three in the Sixers' 121-116 win over the Magic was the final result of that preparation.

"That last play, we ran that play in the first half," Simons said. "We worked it a couple of times, and in the second half we ran that play every single time down the stretch, and it worked every time.

"It was a play we ran through. [It's gratifying] especially when it's working consistently and you're getting different shots out of it and the opposing team just don't know what to do.

"We just continue to run the play and get the open shots we need."

NBC Sports Philadelphia video producer and Sixers Talk podcast co-host Kevin Rice broke down how the Sixers used its different options to keep Orlando guessing.

The action begins with two players stationed near the elbows. A player on the opposite elbow then sets a screen for the player up top, who receives the ball while moving around it.

The Sixers ran the set three consecutive times during the first half. None of the possessions reached the primary option. Each still produced a quality opportunity.

On the first, Joel Embiid cleared LeBron James out of the area and directed Simons around him for a dribble handoff. Simons received an open three-point look.

Orlando took away the handoff the next time down but left Embiid one-on-one against Wendell Carter Jr. Embiid created enough room to retrieve his own miss and convert an and-one opportunity.

The third attempt pulled Orlando's defense into the paint. Two passes moved the ball through the collapsing coverage and created an open three for VJ Edgecombe.

"We had a little thing where he comes flying off behind Joel, and he doesn't get to do that that much, right?" Nurse said of Simons. "So tonight he gets the opportunity to do it, and it was very good for us on a number of plays."

The Sixers changed the action late without abandoning its initial Horns alignment. Simons moved through the lane to help form a Spain pick-and-roll involving James and Embiid.

On the first late possession, Simons remained near the play after James gained a matchup against Carter. Orlando struggled to sort out its assignments, leaving James open for a 19-foot jumper with 54.8 seconds remaining.

After Jalen Suggs made one of two free throws, the Sixers returned to the same setup. This time, Simons relocated to the left corner after James drew Carter on a switch.

James drove into the paint. Suggs lost track of Simons while responding to the penetration, and James sent the ball toward the corner.

Simons buried the open three to give the Sixers a 119-113 lead with 15 seconds left.

"Bron just penetrated the paint," Simons said. "It kind of got some confusion, and when I was on the dunker and kind of spaced out to the corner, I ended up finding myself wide open for the three, and Bron found me."

The Sixers entered the night without Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown, their two leading scorers. Simons made his first start with Philadelphia and recorded 18 points in the city where he grew up.

His final basket also illustrated why Nurse believes the Sixers have more to uncover with him.

"When he's out there, they gotta be really careful," Nurse said. "So I think there's some drives he creates, and just about every time he lets it go, whether he's on balance or not, you think it's going in, and that is a big deal. Like, that is a big deal.

"And again, I think we're still a ways away from really unlocking him to being even more part of some of the things we can do with him to create more shots."

Philadelphia is now 11-0 in clutch games. The finish in Orlando provided a more specific explanation than experience or shot-making alone.

The Sixers installed a set in the morning, explored its counters and trusted it when the game tightened. James supplied the patience to read Orlando's coverage. Embiid occupied the middle of the action. Simons moved without the ball, and Edgecombe remained ready to shoot.

James said his heart rate drops when close games increase the pressure on everyone else.

"I mean, experience," James said. "A guy that's seen every situation this league has to offer. It's nothing I haven't seen. And, you know, in the moments where people's heart rate goes up or anxiety goes up, mine is pretty low. It's actually lower, you know."

The Sixers still have more actions and reads to learn as Nurse becomes familiar with his new personnel, but their latest win showed their innate ability to adapt in big moments.

"I think we're still a long ways away from knowing all the reads and all that stuff because it's just a little early, right?" Nurse said.
